& Before geometry initialization
& \(L_b\), \(b_{\mathrm{in}}\), \(h_b\), sheave angle \(\beta\), and endpoint pulley radii. \\[4pt]

Derived fixed geometry
& Once during geometry initialization
& Center-to-center distance \(C\). \\[4pt]

Dynamic state variables
& Advanced by the ODE integrator
& \(\omega_p\), \(\omega_s\), \(s\), \(\dot{s}\), and \(v_b\). \\[4pt]

State-dependent algebraic quantities
& At each evaluation of the system equations
& \(r_p(s)\), \(r_s(s)\), \(\lambda\), wrap angles, span length, effective radii, and other current geometry quantities. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The calculation has two levels. Geometry initialization first uses the
specified configuration data to establish \(C\). During the simulation, each
evaluation of the system equations begins from the current state. The shift
coordinate gives the primary radius, the fixed belt-length condition gives the
compatible secondary radius, and the remaining belt-path quantities then follow
directly from the current geometry:

\[
\mathbf{x}(t)
\;\longrightarrow\;
s
\;\longrightarrow\;
r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}(s)
\;\longrightarrow\;
r_{s,\mathrm{outer}}(s)
\;\longrightarrow\;
\lambda,\ \phi_p,\ \phi_s,\ \ell
\;\longrightarrow\;
\text{forces, torques, and } \dot{\mathbf{x}}.
\]

The secondary-radius calculation is an algebraic geometry closure within an
evaluation of the system equations; it is not an additional dynamic state.
Once the two radii are known, \(\lambda\), the wrap angles, and the straight-span
length are evaluated directly. A numerical time integrator may evaluate the
system equations multiple times within one time step, so this geometry closure
is performed for each such evaluation rather than only once per accepted time
step.

\section{System State Definition}
\label{sec:state}

The dynamic behavior of the CVT system is described using a set of
\emph{state variables}. A state variable is a quantity whose future
evolution is determined by its current value and the governing
differential equations of the system.

In numerical simulation, state variables are the quantities that are
explicitly integrated forward in time. All other model quantities
— such as torque transfer, transmission ratio, belt forces, and
contact conditions — are computed algebraically from these states.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{State Vector}

\bigeq{eq:state-vector}{State Vector}{
\vect{x}(t) =
\begin{bmatrix}
\omega_p \\
\omega_s \\
s \\
\dot{s} \\
v_b
\end{bmatrix}
}

where:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $\omega_p$ is the angular speed of the primary pulley 
    (\si{\radian\per\second}),

    \item $\omega_s$ is the angular speed of the secondary pulley 
    (\si{\radian\per\second}),

    \item $s$ is the axial shift position of the primary sheave
    (\si{\meter}),

    \item $\dot{s}$ is the axial shift velocity 
    (\si{\meter\per\second}).

    \item $v_b$ is the belt transport speed (\si{\meter\per\second}).
\end{itemize}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Independent Dynamic Degrees of Freedom}

The five state variables above are the dynamic degrees of freedom of the
present state-space model.

The primary pulley can rotate independently under engine input and belt interaction. 
Its angular speed $\omega_p$ therefore represents one rotational degree of freedom.

The secondary pulley also rotates and interacts with both the belt and the external 
drivetrain load. Its angular speed $\omega_s$ represents a second independent 
rotational degree of freedom.

In addition to rotation, the CVT includes axial motion of the primary sheave. 
This axial displacement $s$ determines the effective pulley geometry and therefore 
the transmission ratio. The axial motion is mechanically independent of pure rotation 
and must be modeled explicitly.

Because axial motion follows second-order dynamics, both the axial position $s$ 
and its rate $\dot{s}$ are required to fully describe its evolution.

The quantity $v_b$ is treated as an independent belt-transport degree of
freedom. While detailed internal
belt mechanics remain unresolved, $v_b$ is modeled as a dynamic state whose
evolution is given by an explicit equation of motion derived from torque,
traction, and compatibility relations at the pulley–belt interfaces.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Time Integration Perspective}

During numerical simulation, the state vector is advanced forward in time.

At each timestep $\Delta t$:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The angular speeds $\omega_p$ and $\omega_s$ are updated according 
    to their angular accelerations.

    \item The axial position $s$ is updated using its current velocity $\dot{s}$.

    \item The axial velocity $\dot{s}$ is updated according to the axial acceleration.
    
    \item The belt transport speed $v_b$ is updated according to its angular acceleration.
\end{itemize}

The angular and axial accelerations are determined from the governing 
equations derived in subsequent sections. Once the state vector has been 
updated, all other quantities in the model are computed directly from the 
current values of $\omega_p$, $\omega_s$, $s$, $\dot{s}$, and $v_b$.

\chapter{Derivation}
\section{Pulley Geometry}\label{sec:geometry}

The conceptual overview introduced the CVT as two variable-radius pulleys
connected by a single belt. The primary and secondary pulleys do not choose
their radii independently: when the belt moves outward on one pulley, it must
move inward on the other. The transmission ratio is therefore not a separate
mechanism, but a consequence of where the belt sits on the two pulleys.

This section turns that idea into the geometric and kinematic relationships
needed later in the model. The analysis uses the axial shift position \(s\) 
and the CVT axial direction defined in \Cref{sec:cvt_axial_direction}. 
At this point, the question is not why the CVT shifts. That requires the
mechanical actuation, contact, and drivetrain dynamics developed later. The
question here is more basic:

\[
\text{if } s,\ \dot{s},\ \text{and } \ddot{s} \text{ are known, what are the pulley radii,}
\]
\[
\text{and how are those radii moving?}
\]

The natural place to start is the shape of the pulleys themselves. A belt sitting
in a V-groove has a direct connection between axial motion and radial position.
Since the same belt connects both pulleys, the two pulley radii must also be
related to each other. The goal of this section is to use those geometric facts
to obtain the radius and ratio relationships needed before any forces are
introduced.

Once the position-level geometry is clear, the same relationships can be viewed
as time-varying quantities. If the shift coordinate moves, then the pulley radii
move with it. The remaining kinematic task is therefore to differentiate the
geometric relationships so that radius rates, radius accelerations, and ratio
rate can be expressed consistently from the same foundation.


% ==============================================================
\subsection{Shift Coordinate, Deadzone, and Primary Closure}
\label{sec:shift_definition}

The shift coordinate \(s\) was defined in \Cref{sec:cvt_axial_direction} as the
axial displacement of the movable primary sheave. Since the primary is the
driving pulley on the engine side of the CVT, this primary sheave motion is
used as the reference motion for the ratio change.

The reference position is the fully open primary pulley,

\[
s = 0.
\]

In this position, the primary sheaves are farthest apart and the belt sits at
its smallest primary radius. As \(s\) increases, the movable primary sheave
moves toward the fixed sheave. This closes the primary pulley and moves the CVT
toward upshift.

Two other positions complete the useful range of the coordinate. The first is
the deadzone position \(s_{\mathrm{dz}}\). In the fully open configuration, the
primary gap may be wider than the belt, so the movable sheave can travel a
short distance before it actually begins to squeeze the belt. During this open
region,

\[
0 \le s < s_{\mathrm{dz}},
\]

the primary sheave is moving, but the belt has not yet been forced outward. The
primary radius therefore remains unchanged.

Once the deadzone is taken up,

\[
s_{\mathrm{dz}} \le s,
\]

the belt is seated against the sheave faces. Further closure of the primary
V-groove now forces the belt outward to a larger radius.

The second position is the maximum shift position \(s_{\max}\). This is the
largest possible primary closure. Physically, it corresponds to the end of the
movable sheave travel: the pulley has closed as far as the hardware allows, so
the two sheave components cannot move farther together. The modeled shift range
is therefore

\[
0 \le s \le s_{\max}.
\]

Although the secondary pulley also contains a movable sheave, its axial
position is not denoted by \(s\). Throughout this section, \(s\) refers only
to primary-sheave closure and is used to describe the resulting belt position
on the primary pulley.

% --------------------------------------------------------------

\subsection{Primary Outer Radius as a Function of Shift}
\label{sec:rp}

Since the shift coordinate \(s\) directly measures closure of the primary
pulley, the first geometric relationship to establish is between primary
closure and radial motion of the belt. Before the deadzone is taken up, the
movable sheave can move without changing where the belt sits. Once the belt is
seated between the sheave faces, further closure forces it outward.

This behavior follows from the belt's fixed cross-section. As assumed in
\Cref{assump:fixed-belt-cross-section}, the belt has a fixed axial width and a
fixed radial height. Its fixed width is what prevents it from remaining at the
same radius as the sheaves close: once the gap becomes smaller than the belt,
the belt must move outward along the conical faces.

Consider the instant at which the belt has just become seated, at
\(s=s_{\mathrm{dz}}\). Let \(b_{\mathrm{in}}\) denote the fixed width of the
belt's inner base. At this instant, the sheave gap at the inner base is also
\(b_{\mathrm{in}}\). Now let the movable primary sheave close by a further
amount \(\Delta s\). At the original radial position, the gap is then reduced
to \(b_{\mathrm{in}}-\Delta s\). The belt cannot remain there with the same
width, so it moves outward by \(\Delta r\), as shown in
\Cref{fig:primary_geometry}.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{old_figures/axial_to_radial.png}
\caption{
Primary V-groove geometry after the belt has become seated. A further closure
\(\Delta s\) reduces the gap at the original belt position from
\(b_{\mathrm{in}}\) to \(b_{\mathrm{in}}-\Delta s\). As the belt moves outward
by \(\Delta r\), the angled sheave faces provide an additional axial space
\(x\) on each side.
}
\label{fig:primary_geometry}
\end{figure}

At the belt's new radial position, the two angled sheave faces provide an
additional space \(x\) on either side. The belt still has the same width
\(b_{\mathrm{in}}\), so the available gap must satisfy

\[
b_{\mathrm{in}}-\Delta s+2x=b_{\mathrm{in}}.
\]

Thus,

\[
\Delta s=2x.
\]

The small triangle beside either sheave face relates this side contribution to
the radial rise. For the sheave half-angle \(\beta\),

\[
\tan\beta=\frac{x}{\Delta r},
\]

so that

\[
x=\Delta r\tan\beta.
\]

Substituting this result into the gap relation above gives

\begin{equation}
\Delta r=\frac{\Delta s}{2\tan\beta}.
\label{eq:cone_geometry}
\end{equation}

This is the basic wedge relation for the primary pulley: axial closure of the
sheaves becomes radial motion of the belt. A smaller sheave angle produces more
radial motion for the same axial closure, while a larger sheave angle produces
less.

Taking the infinitesimal limit of \Cref{eq:cone_geometry} yields the local
geometric relationship between axial shift and belt radius,

\begin{equation}
\frac{dr}{ds}=\frac{1}{2\tan\beta}.
\label{eq:cone_geometry_diff}
\end{equation}

Because the sheave face is straight, this derivative is constant, meaning that
radial position varies linearly with axial displacement.

The radial change \(\Delta r\) is the same whether it is measured at the inner
or outer surface of the belt, because the belt's radial height is fixed. The
radii themselves differ by that fixed height. The radius used for the belt-path
geometry is the \emph{primary outer radius},
\(r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}\), measured to the outside surface of the belt. The
corresponding inner radius is

\[
r_{p,\mathrm{inner}}
=
r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}-h_b,
\]

where \(h_b\) is the belt radial height. The effective torque radius is
introduced later.

Let \(r_{p,\mathrm{outer},\min}\) denote the primary outer radius at the fully
open position. The deadzone and the V-groove relation can now be summarized in
one radius map:

\bigeq{eq:primary_radius}{Primary Outer Radius}{
r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}(s)=
\begin{cases}
r_{p,\mathrm{outer},\min}, & 0\le s<s_{\mathrm{dz}}, \\[8pt]
r_{p,\mathrm{outer},\min}
+\dfrac{s-s_{\mathrm{dz}}}{2\tan\beta},
& s_{\mathrm{dz}}\le s\le s_{\max}.
\end{cases}
}

Before the belt is squeezed, the primary radius remains fixed. Once the
deadzone has been taken up, each additional amount of primary closure moves the
belt outward according to the same V-groove geometry.

\subsection{Fixed Belt Length and Pulley Coupling}
\label{sec:belt_length}

The primary-radius map gives the belt position on the driving pulley. The
secondary radius is not chosen independently. The same belt connects both
pulleys, so when the belt moves outward on the primary, its path must adjust on
the secondary.

This coupling follows from two geometric assumptions. First, the belt length is
treated as constant, as stated in \Cref{assump:constant-belt-length}. No matter
where the belt sits on the two pulleys, its complete path around the CVT must
therefore always have the same total length. Second, the idealized belt-path assumption in \Cref{assump:idealized-belt-path}
describes how that path is measured. Within each pulley contact region, the belt
follows a circular arc at a uniform radius, while the two sections between the
pulleys are straight tangent spans. Together, these assumptions mean that any
proposed pair of pulley radii defines a belt path with a particular length. The
geometrically allowable pair is the one whose path length equals the fixed belt
length.

For compactness, the radii in this subsection are written as
\(r_p\) and \(r_s\), with both measured to the same reference surface of the
belt. In the present geometry model, these are the primary and secondary outer
radii introduced above. The corresponding belt length \(L_b\) is referenced to
that same outer surface.

With those radii fixed at a given instant, the assumed belt path is shown in
\Cref{fig:belt_geometry}. It is made up of an arc around the primary pulley, an
arc around the secondary pulley, and two equal straight spans between them.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{./illustrations/belt_length_geometry.jpg}
\caption{
Idealized open-belt geometry for the two-pulley CVT. The belt follows circular
contact arcs of radii \(r_p\) and \(r_s\), connected by two straight tangent
spans of length \(\ell\). The pulley centers are separated by the fixed distance
\(C\), while \(\phi_p\) and \(\phi_s\) denote the corresponding wrap angles.
}
\label{fig:belt_geometry}
\end{figure}

The amount of wrap on each pulley depends on how the straight spans tilt between
the two radii. Let \(\lambda\) denote the signed angle between the line of
centers and either straight span, as shown in \Cref{fig:belt_geometry}. The
right-triangle geometry gives

\begin{equation}
\sin\lambda
=
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C},
\label{eq:lambda_def}
\end{equation}

or equivalently,

\begin{equation}
\lambda
=
\sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}\right).
\label{eq:lambda_asin}
\end{equation}

When the secondary radius is larger than the primary radius, \(\lambda>0\).
The belt then wraps less than half a turn around the smaller primary pulley and
more than half a turn around the larger secondary pulley. The resulting wrap
angles are

\begin{align}
\phi_p &= \pi-2\lambda,
\label{eq:wrap_primary}\\
\phi_s &= \pi+2\lambda.
\label{eq:wrap_secondary}
\end{align}

If the primary becomes larger than the secondary, \(\lambda\) becomes negative.
The same expressions then reverse the wrap distribution automatically.

The same triangle also gives the length of either straight span,

\begin{equation}
\ell
=
\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}.
\label{eq:span_length}
\end{equation}

The total geometric belt length is therefore the sum of the two contact arcs
and the two straight spans,

\begin{equation}
L_b
=
r_p\phi_p+r_s\phi_s+2\ell.
\label{eq:belt_length_general}
\end{equation}

Substituting \Cref{eq:wrap_primary,eq:wrap_secondary,eq:span_length} into
\Cref{eq:belt_length_general} gives

\begin{align}
L_b
&=
r_p(\pi-2\lambda)
+
r_s(\pi+2\lambda)
+
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}
\nonumber\\
&=
\pi(r_p+r_s)
+
2\lambda(r_s-r_p)
+
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}.
\label{eq:belt_length_lambda}
\end{align}

Finally, substituting \Cref{eq:lambda_asin} gives the belt-length relation
entirely in terms of the two radii and the center distance,

\begin{equation}
L_b
=
\pi(r_p+r_s)
+
2(r_s-r_p)
\sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}\right)
+
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}.
\label{eq:belt_length_exact}
\end{equation}

This arc-and-tangent construction is commonly used in belt-drive geometry to
relate pulley radii, wrap angles, and belt length; \citet{BudynasNisbett2015}
presents the same underlying construction for conventional belt drives.

\Cref{eq:belt_length_exact} couples the two pulley radii and the
center-to-center distance through the fixed belt length. The primary radius is
available from the shift coordinate, but the secondary radius and
center-to-center distance are still unknown.

Neither quantity can be isolated directly from
\Cref{eq:belt_length_exact}, since each appears both outside and inside the
inverse-sine and square-root terms. The remaining geometry must therefore be
established from this implicit belt-length relation.

\begin{remarkbox}[Remark (Simplified Belt-Length Relation)]

The exact belt-length relation does not permit a closed-form solution for the
unknown geometry. A common simplification, appropriate when the pulley-radius
difference is small relative to the center distance, is to neglect the
wrap-angle correction by taking

\[
\lambda
=
\sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}\right)
\approx 0.
\]

\Cref{eq:belt_length_exact} then becomes

\[
L_b
\approx
\pi(r_p+r_s)
+
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2},
\]

which can be rearranged algebraically to obtain the unknown geometry in closed
form. The present model retains the exact relation; the effect of this
simplification is examined in \Cref{sec:approximation-validation}.

\end{remarkbox}

\subsection{Secondary Radius from Belt-Length Compatibility}
\label{sec:secondary_radius}

The two remaining quantities do not need to be found simultaneously. The
center-to-center distance is fixed by the assembled CVT, whereas the secondary
radius changes as the sheaves shift. Thus, before using the belt-length relation
to follow the changing secondary radius, the fixed shaft spacing can be
established once from any configuration in which both pulley radii are known.

A natural choice is the configuration at the beginning of primary-sheave
travel. The primary pulley is fully open and the secondary pulley is fully
closed, so the belt sits at the bottom of the primary groove and at the top of
the secondary groove. Its two radii are therefore known directly:

\[
r_p=r_{p,\mathrm{outer},\min},
\qquad
r_s=r_{s,\mathrm{outer},\max}.
\]

With this reference configuration and the specified belt length, the implicit
belt-length relation contains only the fixed center-to-center distance (C) as
an unknown.

\paragraph{Reference center-to-center distance}

Substituting the known reference radii into the belt-length relation leaves one
unknown: the center-to-center distance \(C\). Since \(C\) appears both directly
and inside the inverse-sine and square-root terms, it cannot be rearranged into
an explicit expression. Instead, the belt-length condition is written as a
residual:

\bigeq{eq:belt_length_residual}{Belt-Length Compatibility Residual}{
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}(r_p,r_s,C)
=
\pi(r_p+r_s)
+
2(r_s-r_p)
\sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}\right)
+
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}
-
L_b.
}

For any proposed geometry, this residual is the difference between the belt
path required by that geometry and the available belt length. A positive value
means that the required path is too long for the belt, while a negative value
means that it is too short. At the reference radii, the assembled
center-to-center distance is therefore the value of \(C\) for which

\begin{equation}
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}
\!\left(
r_{p,\mathrm{outer},\min},
r_{s,\mathrm{outer},\max},
C
\right)
=0.
\label{eq:center_distance_constraint}
\end{equation}

This is a one-dimensional nonlinear root-finding problem. The center distance
is found numerically over its geometrically admissible range until the
belt-length residual vanishes. Details of the numerical procedure, including
the search interval and convergence criterion, are given in
\Cref{app:geometry-root-solve}. Because this calculation uses only fixed belt
and sheave geometry, it is performed once during geometry initialization. The
resulting value of \(C\) then remains fixed throughout the simulation.

\begin{remarkbox}[Remark (Dependence of the Reference Geometry)]
At the reference configuration, the belt length \(L_b\), center-to-center
distance \(C\), and the two pulley radii are geometrically dependent. Once any
three of these quantities are specified, the belt-length relation determines
the fourth. The initialization solve for \(C\) therefore makes explicit the
dependence of the assembled center distance on the selected belt and endpoint
pulley geometry.

A mathematical solution does not by itself guarantee that the resulting
assembly is physically realizable. For example, the implied center distance may
cause sheave, belt, or housing interference, or may lie outside the available
package. Such constraints must be checked against the physical CVT design
separately.
\end{remarkbox}

\paragraph{Secondary radius during shift}

With the center-to-center distance established, the original problem has only
one remaining unknown. At any shift position,
\Cref{eq:primary_radius} gives \(r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}(s)\), and the secondary
outer radius is the value that makes the same fixed belt fit around both
pulleys:

\bigeq{eq:secondary_radius}{Secondary Outer Radius}{
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}
\!\left(
r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}(s),
r_{s,\mathrm{outer}}(s),
C
\right)
=0.
}

For a given shift position, \Cref{eq:secondary_radius} is again a
one-dimensional nonlinear root-finding problem, now in
\(r_{s,\mathrm{outer}}(s)\). The search is restricted to the physical range of
secondary pulley radii, and the admissible root gives the belt position on the
secondary pulley. The same numerical procedure described in
\Cref{app:geometry-root-solve} is used whenever the system equations are
evaluated as the shift coordinate changes.

\subsection{Pitch Radius and Geometric CVT Ratio}
\label{sec:pitch_radius_and_ratio}

The two outer radii now locate the belt within both pulley grooves and define
the outer belt path. This is sufficient for the belt-length geometry, including
the wrap angles and straight-span length derived in \Cref{sec:belt_length}.

The next quantities of interest concern belt motion and torque transfer. For
these purposes, the outer belt surface is not the appropriate reference. The
longitudinal tension in a rubber V-belt is carried by its reinforcement cords,
so the radius associated with belt transport and the tension moment arm lies at
the cord layer rather than at the outer surface.

The radius of the cord layer is commonly called the \emph{pitch radius}, or
\emph{cord radius}. \citet{Gerbert1996} identifies the pitch radius with the
cord radius and uses it to relate belt motion and transmitted torque.

Let \(d_{\mathrm{cord}}\) denote the fixed radial distance from the outer belt
surface to the centre of the cord layer, where

\[
0 \leq d_{\mathrm{cord}} \leq h_b.
\]

The primary and secondary pitch radii are therefore

\begin{align}
r_{p,\mathrm{pitch}}(s)
&=
r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}(s)-d_{\mathrm{cord}},
\label{eq:primary_pitch_radius}\\[4pt]
r_{s,\mathrm{pitch}}(s)
&=
r_{s,\mathrm{outer}}(s)-d_{\mathrm{cord}}.
\label{eq:secondary_pitch_radius}
\end{align}

Because the same belt offset is applied at both pulleys,

\[
r_{s,\mathrm{pitch}}(s)-r_{p,\mathrm{pitch}}(s)
=
r_{s,\mathrm{outer}}(s)-r_{p,\mathrm{outer}}(s).
\]

The tangency angle, wrap angles, and straight-span length are therefore
unchanged by this change of reference radius. The pitch radii are introduced
only to describe the belt's transport motion and its torque moment arms.

The two pitch radii also describe the effective gearing associated with the
belt's current position. Define the geometric CVT ratio as

\bigeq{eq:cvt_ratio}{Geometric CVT Ratio}{
R_g(s)
=
\frac{r_{s,\mathrm{pitch}}(s)}
     {r_{p,\mathrm{pitch}}(s)}.
}

This ratio describes the pulley leverage made available by the current belt
position. A large secondary pitch radius together with a small primary pitch
radius gives a large reduction ratio. As the primary sheave closes, the belt
moves outward on the primary and inward on the secondary, causing
\(R_g(s)\) to decrease.

Unlike a pair of rigid meshed gears, however, the belt does not automatically
transfer motion and torque through this exact ratio. The geometric ratio
describes the gearing available from the pulley geometry; whether that gearing
is realized depends on the belt maintaining sufficient traction at the pulley
contacts.

\subsection{Pulley-Radius Rates and Accelerations}
\label{sec:pulley_radius_rates}

The geometry above determines where the belt sits on each pulley at a given
shift position \(s\). When the primary sheave begins to move, those belt
positions must move as well. The next quantities to establish are therefore the
primary and secondary radial rates, \(\dot r_p\) and \(\dot r_s\), followed by
their radial accelerations, \(\ddot r_p\) and \(\ddot r_s\).

The fixed belt-length geometry was written using the outer belt radii, while
belt transport and the geometric CVT ratio use the pitch radii. For the rate
relations, that distinction disappears. For either pulley \(j\in\{p,s\}\),

\begin{equation}
r_{j,\mathrm{pitch}}
=
r_{j,\mathrm{outer}}-d_{\mathrm{cord}}.
\label{eq:pitch_outer_radius_offset}
\end{equation}

The cord offset is fixed for the selected belt. Differentiating with respect to
\(s\) or time therefore removes it:

\begin{align}
\frac{d r_{j,\mathrm{pitch}}}{ds}
&=
\frac{d r_{j,\mathrm{outer}}}{ds},
\label{eq:pitch_outer_shift_rate_equivalence}\\[4pt]
\dot r_{j,\mathrm{pitch}}
&=
\dot r_{j,\mathrm{outer}},
\label{eq:pitch_outer_time_rate_equivalence}\\[4pt]
\ddot r_{j,\mathrm{pitch}}
&=
\ddot r_{j,\mathrm{outer}}.
\label{eq:pitch_outer_acceleration_equivalence}
\end{align}

Thus, the same radial rates and accelerations apply whichever reference surface
is used. To avoid carrying repeated subscripts, \(r_p\) and \(r_s\) denote the
primary and secondary radii throughout the remainder of this section.

\subsubsection{Primary Radius Rate and Acceleration}
\label{sec:primary_radius_rates}

The primary radius is already known explicitly from the primary sheave
geometry. Recalling \Cref{eq:primary_radius},

\begin{equation}
r_p(s)
=
\begin{cases}
r_{p,\min},
& 0\leq s<s_{\mathrm{dz}},\\[8pt]
\displaystyle
r_{p,\min}+\frac{s-s_{\mathrm{dz}}}{2\tan\beta},
& s_{\mathrm{dz}}\leq s\leq s_{\max}.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:primary_radius_recalled}
\end{equation}

The primary radius changes in time only because the shift coordinate changes in
time. Using the chain rule,

\begin{equation}
\dot r_p
=
\frac{d r_p}{ds}\dot s.
\label{eq:primary_radius_rate_chain}
\end{equation}

The derivative \(dr_p/ds\) comes directly from
\Cref{eq:primary_radius_recalled}. In the dead zone, changing \(s\) does not yet
move the belt radially. Once the sheave faces engage the belt, the conical
geometry gives a constant radial change for each unit of axial shift:

\begin{equation}
\frac{d r_p}{ds}
=
\begin{cases}
0,
& 0\leq s<s_{\mathrm{dz}},\\[10pt]
\displaystyle
\frac{1}{2\tan\beta},
& s_{\mathrm{dz}}<s\leq s_{\max}.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:primary_radius_shift_derivative}
\end{equation}

Substituting this result into \Cref{eq:primary_radius_rate_chain} gives

\bigeq{eq:primary_radius_rate}{Primary Radius Rate}{
\dot r_p
=
\begin{cases}
0,
& 0\leq s<s_{\mathrm{dz}},\\[10pt]
\displaystyle
\frac{\dot s}{2\tan\beta},
& s_{\mathrm{dz}}<s\leq s_{\max}.
\end{cases}
}

To obtain the radial acceleration, differentiate
\Cref{eq:primary_radius_rate_chain} once more. This is a product of
\(dr_p/ds\) and \(\dot s\), so use the product rule:

\begin{align}
\ddot r_p
&=
\frac{d}{dt}
\left(
\frac{d r_p}{ds}\dot s
\right)
\nonumber\\
&=
\frac{d}{dt}
\left(
\frac{d r_p}{ds}
\right)\dot s
+
\frac{d r_p}{ds}\ddot s
\nonumber\\
&=
\frac{d^2r_p}{ds^2}\dot s^2
+
\frac{d r_p}{ds}\ddot s.
\label{eq:primary_radius_acceleration_chain}
\end{align}

Within either smooth branch of \Cref{eq:primary_radius_recalled}, the
slope \(dr_p/ds\) is constant. Hence \(d^2r_p/ds^2=0\), leaving

\bigeq{eq:primary_radius_acceleration}{Primary Radius Acceleration}{
\ddot r_p
=
\begin{cases}
0,
& 0\leq s<s_{\mathrm{dz}},\\[10pt]
\displaystyle
\frac{\ddot s}{2\tan\beta},
& s_{\mathrm{dz}}<s\leq s_{\max}.
\end{cases}
}

\begin{remarkbox}[Remark (Dead-Zone Boundary)]
The idealized primary-radius map changes slope at \(s=s_{\mathrm{dz}}\), so
its ordinary second derivative is not defined at that single point. The
expressions above apply within the dead-zone and active-shift branches; the
boundary is handled as a regime transition.
\end{remarkbox}

\subsubsection{Secondary Radius Rate}
\label{sec:secondary_radius_rate}

The secondary radius is not prescribed directly by the shift coordinate. It is
the radius required to keep the fixed belt compatible with the current primary
radius. Its rate must therefore come from differentiating the belt-length
compatibility condition itself.

Recall the residual from \Cref{eq:belt_length_residual}:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}(r_p,r_s,C)
={}&
\pi(r_p+r_s)
+
2(r_s-r_p)
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)\\
&+
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}
-L_b
=
0.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:belt_length_residual_recalled}
\end{equation}

Here, \(C\) and \(L_b\) remain fixed, while \(r_p\) and \(r_s\) can both vary
with time. Using the multivariable chain rule,

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}}{\partial r_p}\dot r_p
+
\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}}{\partial r_s}\dot r_s
=
0.
\label{eq:secondary_radius_rate_constraint}
\end{equation}

The two partial derivatives tell us how the required belt-path length changes
when one pulley radius changes while the other radius and the center distance
are held fixed. We find them next.

\paragraph{Partial derivative with respect to \(r_p\)}

Start with the inverse-sine contribution. It is a product, so its derivative
contains one term from differentiating \(r_s-r_p\) and a second from
differentiating the inverse-sine factor:

\begin{align}
&
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_p}
\left[
(r_s-r_p)
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
\right]
\nonumber\\
={}&
\frac{\partial(r_s-r_p)}{\partial r_p}
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
+
(r_s-r_p)
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_p}
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right).
\label{eq:product_rule_asin_primary_start}
\end{align}

For the first factor, \(r_s\) is held fixed:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial(r_s-r_p)}{\partial r_p}
=
-1.
\label{eq:linear_difference_primary_derivative}
\end{equation}

For the inverse-sine factor, the chain rule gives

\begin{align}
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_p}
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
&=
-\frac{1/C}
{\sqrt{
1-\left(\dfrac{r_s-r_p}{C}\right)^2
}}
\nonumber\\
&=
-\frac{1}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:asin_primary_derivative}
\end{align}

Substituting the two derivatives into \Cref{eq:product_rule_asin_primary_start} gives

\begin{align}
&
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_p}
\left[
(r_s-r_p)
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
\right]
\nonumber\\
={}&
-\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
-
\frac{r_s-r_p}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:product_rule_asin_primary_final}
\end{align}

The remaining nonlinear contribution comes from the straight spans. Using the
chain rule,

\begin{align}
&
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_p}
\left[
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}
\right]
\nonumber\\
={}&
2
\left[
\frac{1}
{2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}
\right]
\left[
2(r_s-r_p)
\right]
\nonumber\\
={}&
\frac{2(r_s-r_p)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:square_root_primary_derivative}
\end{align}

We can now assemble the derivative of the full residual. The terms involving
the straight-span square root appear with equal magnitude and opposite sign:

\begin{align}
\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}}{\partial r_p}
={}&
\pi
+
2\left[
-\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
-
\frac{r_s-r_p}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}
\right]
+
\frac{2(r_s-r_p)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}
\nonumber\\
={}&
\pi
-
2\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
\nonumber\\
={}&
\phi_p.
\label{eq:belt_residual_primary_partial}
\end{align}

\paragraph{Partial derivative with respect to \(r_s\)}

The calculation with respect to \(r_s\) follows the same steps. The only
difference is that increasing \(r_s\) increases the radius difference
\(r_s-r_p\), rather than decreasing it. Thus,

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial(r_s-r_p)}{\partial r_s}
=
1,
\qquad
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_s}
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
=
\frac{1}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:secondary_partial_component_derivatives}
\end{equation}

Using these signs in the same product-rule calculation gives

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_s}
\left[
(r_s-r_p)
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
\right]
=
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
+
\frac{r_s-r_p}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:product_rule_asin_secondary_final}
\end{equation}

The straight-span contribution changes sign as well:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial}{\partial r_s}
\left[
2\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}
\right]
=
-\frac{2(r_s-r_p)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:square_root_secondary_derivative}
\end{equation}

Assembling the full derivative gives

\begin{align}
\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}}{\partial r_s}
={}&
\pi
+
2\left[
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
+
\frac{r_s-r_p}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}
\right]
-
\frac{2(r_s-r_p)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}
\nonumber\\
={}&
\pi
+
2\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right)
\nonumber\\
={}&
\phi_s.
\label{eq:belt_residual_secondary_partial}
\end{align}

Again, the square-root terms cancel. The two sensitivities are therefore the
two wrap angles:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}}{\partial r_p}
=
\phi_p,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{belt}}}{\partial r_s}
=
\phi_s.
\label{eq:belt_residual_partials_as_wrap_angles}
\end{equation}

Substituting these results into \Cref{eq:secondary_radius_rate_constraint}
gives

\begin{equation}
\phi_p\dot r_p+\phi_s\dot r_s=0.
\label{eq:wrap_weighted_radius_rate_balance}
\end{equation}

This says that, under fixed belt length, the two radial motions are weighted by
their current wrap angles. Solving for the secondary radius rate,

\begin{equation}
\dot r_s
=
-\frac{\phi_p}{\phi_s}\dot r_p.
\label{eq:secondary_radius_rate_general}
\end{equation}

Finally, inserting the primary radius rate from
\Cref{eq:primary_radius_rate} gives

\bigeq{eq:secondary_radius_rate}{Secondary Radius Rate}{
\dot r_s
=
\begin{cases}
0,
& 0\leq s<s_{\mathrm{dz}},\\[12pt]
\displaystyle
-\frac{\phi_p}{\phi_s}
\frac{\dot s}{2\tan\beta},
& s_{\mathrm{dz}}<s\leq s_{\max}.
\end{cases}
}

The negative sign is the geometric consequence of the fixed belt length: as the
belt moves outward on the primary pulley, it must move inward on the secondary
pulley.

\subsubsection{Secondary Radius Acceleration}
\label{sec:secondary_radius_acceleration}

The secondary radius rate is not constant while the CVT shifts, because the
wrap angles themselves change as the belt moves. To find \(\ddot r_s\), first
find the rates of the wrap angles appearing in
\Cref{eq:wrap_weighted_radius_rate_balance}.

The two wrap angles are determined by the tangency angle,

\[
\lambda
=
\sin^{-1}\!\left(
\frac{r_s-r_p}{C}
\right).
\]

Using the chain rule,

\begin{align}
\dot\lambda
&=
\frac{\dot r_s-\dot r_p}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:tangency_angle_rate}
\end{align}

Since \(\phi_p=\pi-2\lambda\) and \(\phi_s=\pi+2\lambda\),

\begin{align}
\dot\phi_p
&=
-2\dot\lambda
=
\frac{2(\dot r_p-\dot r_s)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}},
\label{eq:primary_wrap_angle_rate}\\[6pt]
\dot\phi_s
&=
2\dot\lambda
=
-\frac{2(\dot r_p-\dot r_s)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:secondary_wrap_angle_rate}
\end{align}

Now differentiate the rate balance,
\(\phi_p\dot r_p+\phi_s\dot r_s=0\). Each term is the product of a changing
wrap angle and a changing radius rate, so use the product rule on both:

\begin{align}
0
&=
\frac{d}{dt}
\left(
\phi_p\dot r_p+\phi_s\dot r_s
\right)
\nonumber\\
&=
\dot\phi_p\dot r_p
+
\phi_p\ddot r_p
+
\dot\phi_s\dot r_s
+
\phi_s\ddot r_s.
\label{eq:wrap_weighted_radius_acceleration_start}
\end{align}

Substituting the wrap-angle rates from
\Cref{eq:primary_wrap_angle_rate,eq:secondary_wrap_angle_rate} gives

\begin{align}
0
={}&
\frac{2(\dot r_p-\dot r_s)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}\dot r_p
+
\phi_p\ddot r_p
-
\frac{2(\dot r_p-\dot r_s)}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}\dot r_s
+
\phi_s\ddot r_s
\nonumber\\
={}&
\phi_p\ddot r_p
+
\phi_s\ddot r_s
+
\frac{2(\dot r_p-\dot r_s)^2}
{\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}.
\label{eq:wrap_weighted_radius_acceleration_balance}
\end{align}

The final term is present because a changing radius changes the wrap angles as
well as the radius itself. Solving for the secondary radial acceleration gives

\begin{equation}
\ddot r_s
=
-\frac{\phi_p}{\phi_s}\ddot r_p
-
\frac{2}
{\phi_s\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}
\left(\dot r_p-\dot r_s\right)^2.
\label{eq:secondary_radius_acceleration_general}
\end{equation}

The remaining difference of radius rates can be written using the result
already obtained in \Cref{eq:secondary_radius_rate_general}:

\begin{align}
\dot r_p-\dot r_s
&=
\dot r_p
+
\frac{\phi_p}{\phi_s}\dot r_p
\nonumber\\
&=
\frac{\phi_p+\phi_s}{\phi_s}\dot r_p
\nonumber\\
&=
\frac{2\pi}{\phi_s}\dot r_p.
\label{eq:radius_rate_difference}
\end{align}

Substituting this into \Cref{eq:secondary_radius_acceleration_general} gives

\begin{equation}
\ddot r_s
=
-\frac{\phi_p}{\phi_s}\ddot r_p
-
\frac{8\pi^2}
{\phi_s^3\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}}
\dot r_p^2.
\label{eq:secondary_radius_acceleration_primary_form}
\end{equation}

Finally, insert the primary radius rate and acceleration from
\Cref{eq:primary_radius_rate,eq:primary_radius_acceleration}:

\bigeq{eq:secondary_radius_acceleration}{Secondary Radius Acceleration}{
\ddot r_s
=
\begin{cases}
0,
& 0\leq s<s_{\mathrm{dz}},\\[14pt]
\displaystyle
-\frac{\phi_p}{\phi_s}
\frac{\ddot s}{2\tan\beta}
-
\frac{2\pi^2}
{\phi_s^3
\sqrt{C^2-(r_s-r_p)^2}
\tan^2\beta}
\dot s^2,
& s_{\mathrm{dz}}<s\leq s_{\max}.
\end{cases}
}

\begin{remarkbox}[Remark (Rate of the Geometric CVT Ratio)]
The individual pulley-radius rates and accelerations are the quantities needed
in the subsequent development. They can also be combined to determine how
quickly the geometric CVT ratio changes. This result is not required in the
main derivation, but it gives a useful design-level view of how sheave angle and
current pulley geometry shape the available ratio-change rate. The derivation
is given in \Cref{app:ratio-rate}.
\end{remarkbox}

\paragraph{Relation to prior transient models}

The radius-rate and radius-acceleration relations above follow from the
particular global belt-path representation adopted here: fixed belt length and
center-to-center distance, one nominal radius over each pulley contact arc, and
straight tangent spans between the pulleys. Within this representation, the
shift coordinate \(s\) determines the primary radius, while the belt-length
condition determines the compatible secondary radius. Differentiating that same
condition then gives the coupled radius rates and accelerations required by the
remainder of the model.

\citet{DuanEtAl2016} develop a closely related chain-CVT geometry, including
the open-belt arc-and-tangent path and the wedge relation between
movable-sheave axial motion and nominal radius motion. Their formulation
retains an additional elastic path-length degree of freedom: chain extension
and its rate contribute to span tension, while pulley deformation produces a
local difference between the nominal pulley radius and the radius followed by
an individual chain element. The present formulation instead holds the nominal
belt path at fixed length and does not resolve a spatial deformation field.
Within those assumptions, it provides the compatible nominal radius pair and
its rates directly from
\[
s,\ \dot s,\ \ddot s
\quad\longrightarrow\quad
r_p,\ \dot r_p,\ \ddot r_p,\ 
r_s,\ \dot r_s,\ \ddot r_s.
\]
The corresponding tradeoff is that elastic path-length change and
deformation-induced local radius variation are excluded from this geometric
layer.

Rubber-belt transient models use more than one representation of radius
evolution. Analytical shift-mechanics models, such as those of
\citet{Sorge2008} and \citet{CammalleriSorge2009}, describe local belt
migration through a pulley wrap, coupling radial motion to circumferential
transport, sliding, and contact-region mechanics. Discretized rubber-belt
models, such as those of \citet{JulioPlante2011} and \citet{Ballew2015}, obtain the
evolving belt geometry from the motion and contact state of individual belt
elements. Those approaches can resolve local belt deformation, tension
redistribution, and contact motion in greater detail, but require additional
spatial or nodal states and associated contact laws.

The present model does not attempt to reproduce that internal belt trajectory.
It instead uses the global belt-path geometry to provide the compatible nominal
pulley radii and their derivatives from the single primary shift coordinate.
This lower-order closure is useful here because those quantities enter directly
into the subsequent belt-transport, traction, and rotational equations.


\subsection{Section Conclusion}
\label{sec:geometry_conclusion}

The pulley geometry is now closed by a single shift coordinate. At any
admissible value of \(s\), the primary sheave geometry fixes the primary belt
radius, while fixed belt length determines the compatible secondary radius.
Together, these radii determine the belt path, the pitch radii, and the
geometric ratio made available by the current pulley arrangement.

The same geometry also determines how these quantities move when the shift
coordinate changes. A primary sheave displacement produces a corresponding
primary radial motion, while the fixed belt length constrains the secondary
radius to respond with it. The resulting radius rates and accelerations are
therefore not separate assumptions; they follow from the same belt-path
geometry that determines the instantaneous ratio.

This completes the geometric part of the model. The remaining development can
treat the pulley radii, belt path, and their motion as a consistent consequence
of the current shift state, rather than as independent quantities.


\section{Pulley Axial Force Models}\label{sec:axial}

The geometric relations developed in the previous section define how
the transmission ratio varies with axial shift. What remains is to
determine \emph{why} the shift coordinate $s(t)$ changes in time.

Axial motion of the sheaves is driven by clamping forces generated
within the primary and secondary assemblies. On the primary side,
centrifugal flyweights and springs produce an inward axial force
that tends to close the sheaves and increase radius. On the secondary
side, torque reaction and spring preload generate an opposing axial
force.

The evolution of the shift coordinate is governed by the
net axial force acting on the movable sheave assembly.
Applying Newton's second law in the axial direction
(\theoryRef{th:newton-trans}) gives

\begin{equation}
m \, \ddot{s}
=
F_{p,\text{ax}} - F_{s,\text{ax}},
\label{eq:axial_newton_base}
\end{equation}

where $F_{p,\text{ax}}$ and $F_{s,\text{ax}}$ denote the axial forces
generated by the primary and secondary mechanisms, respectively,
and $m$ is the effective axial inertia of the moving
components.

The purpose of this section is to derive expressions for
$F_{p,\text{ax}}$ and $F_{s,\text{ax}}$ as functions of the current
system state. Once these force models are established, the shift
acceleration $\ddot s$ follows directly, completing the dynamic
description of axial motion.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.8\textwidth]{illustrations/pulleyForces/cvt_overview_forces.png}
\caption{
Axial force balance governing sheave motion. Primary and secondary
mechanisms generate opposing axial forces. The net force determines
the shift acceleration $\ddot s$.
}
\label{fig:axial_force_balance}
\end{figure}

Although the specific actuator mechanisms modeled here correspond
to a flyweight-driven primary and a torque-reactive helix secondary,
the formulation is modular: any actuator model may be substituted
provided it returns the net axial force as a function of state.


% ==============================================================
\subsection{Ramp Geometry Parameterization}\label{sec:ramp-geometry}

Both pulley actuators redirect forces through inclined contact surfaces.
As the shift coordinate $s$ evolves, mechanical elements slide along
these surfaces, converting radial or circumferential forces into axial
clamping force.

To keep the formulation general and modular, the ramp geometries are
parameterized directly as functions of the axial shift coordinate $s$.
Any actuator profile may therefore be substituted provided it supplies
the appropriate geometric mapping and derivative.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Primary Ramp Geometry (Axial-to-Radial Mapping)}

On the primary pulley, centrifugal force acting on the flyweights
is redirected into axial closing force through a ramp surface
(see \Fig{fig:primary_ramp}). As the movable sheave translates
axially, the flyweight slides along this ramp.

We represent the ramp by an axial-to-radial profile
\begin{equation}
r_f = r_f(s),
\label{eq:primary_profile}
\end{equation}
where $r_f(s)$ is the effective flyweight radius.

An incremental axial displacement $ds$ produces a radial displacement
$dr_f$. The local slope of the ramp is therefore $dr_f/ds$.
The instantaneous primary ramp angle is denoted by $\alpha_p(s)$ and is
defined with respect to the axial direction as
\begin{equation}
\tan\alpha_p(s) = \frac{dr_f}{ds},
\qquad
\alpha_p(s) = \tan^{-1}\!\left(\frac{dr_f}{ds}\right).
\label{eq:alpha_primary_from_slope}
\end{equation}

This definition follows directly from the geometry of the sliding
motion: a steeper ramp (larger $dr_f/ds$) produces greater radial
change per unit axial travel and therefore greater axial gain
when radial force is redirected through the surface.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/primary_ramp_definition.png}
\end{minipage}
\hfill
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/radius_wrt_s_function.png}
\end{minipage}
\caption{
Primary ramp geometry.
\textbf{Left:} As axial displacement $s$ increases, the flyweight slides along
the ramp, producing radial change $dr_f$. The instantaneous ramp angle satisfies
$\tan\alpha_p = dr_f/ds$.
\textbf{Right:} Example profiles of $r_f(s)$ and its slope $dr_f/ds$.
}
\label{fig:primary_ramp}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Primary Admissibility Conditions}

In order to be a valid primary ramp profile $r_f(s)$, it must satisfy:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item $r_f(s) \in C^1([0,s_{\max}])$ (continuously differentiable),
    \item $\dfrac{dr_f}{ds} \ge 0$ over $[0, s_{\max}]$,
    \item $\dfrac{dr_f}{ds}$ is finite over $[0, s_{\max}]$.
\end{enumerate}

Condition (1) ensures the ramp angle $\alpha_p(s)$ is well-defined and
continuous. Condition (2) guarantees that increasing axial displacement
does not reduce flyweight radius. Condition (3) ensures the ramp angle
remains strictly below $\pi/2$ and the axial gain $\tan\alpha_p$ remains finite.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Secondary Helix Geometry (Axial-to-Rotation Mapping)}

On the secondary pulley, axial travel produces relative rotation
of a helical ramp mechanism, loading a torsional spring and generating
torque-reactive clamping (see \Fig{fig:secondary_helix_def}).

We parameterize this kinematically as
\begin{equation}
\theta_s = \theta_s(s),
\label{eq:secondary_theta_profile}
\end{equation}
where $\theta_s(s)$ is the helix-induced rotation.

An incremental rotation $d\theta_s$ corresponds to a circumferential
displacement $r_h d\theta_s$ along a cylindrical surface of effective
radius $r_h$. The instantaneous secondary helix angle is denoted by
$\alpha_s(s)$ and is defined from the local slope of the ramp surface:

\[
\tan\alpha_s
=
\frac{\text{axial rise}}{\text{circumferential run}}
=
\frac{ds}{r_h d\theta_s}.
\]

Rewriting in differential form gives
\begin{equation}
\tan\alpha_s(s)
=
\frac{1}{r_h \, \dfrac{d\theta_s}{ds}}.
\label{eq:alpha_secondary_inverse_slope}
\end{equation}

Thus, a shallower helix (smaller $\alpha_s$) produces greater rotation
per unit axial travel, while a steeper helix produces less rotation.
This inverse relationship arises naturally from the geometry of motion
on a cylindrical surface.

\begin{remarkbox}[Remark (Contrast with Primary Ramp)]
The primary ramp behaves like a simple wedge: radial displacement is
directly proportional to axial travel, so the ramp angle is naturally
defined by $\tan\alpha_p = dr_f/ds$.

The secondary mechanism instead behaves like a power screw.
Axial motion is produced through rotation around a cylindrical surface,
so the relevant geometric ratio is how much rotation occurs per unit
axial travel. This leads to the slope definition
$ds/(r_h d\theta_s)$ and the inverse relationship in
\Eq{eq:alpha_secondary_inverse_slope}.

In short, the primary redirects force through a wedge,
while the secondary converts torque through a screw-like helix.
The difference is purely geometric and reflects the distinct
mechanisms by which axial motion is generated.
\end{remarkbox}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/secondary_helix_torque_to_axial.png}
\caption{
Secondary helix geometry. Axial travel $s$ induces rotation
$\theta_s(s)$ around a cylindrical surface of radius $r_h$.
The instantaneous helix angle satisfies
$\tan\alpha_s = ds/(r_h d\theta_s)$.
}
\label{fig:secondary_helix_def}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Secondary Admissibility Conditions}

The helix rotation mapping $\theta_s(s)$ must satisfy:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\theta_s(s) \in C^1([0,s_{\max}])$ (continuously differentiable),
    \item $\dfrac{d\theta_s}{ds} \ge 0$ over $[0, s_{\max}]$,
    \item $\dfrac{d\theta_s}{ds}$ is finite over $[0, s_{\max}]$.
\end{enumerate}

Condition (1) ensures the torque-to-axial conversion remains continuous.
Condition (2) guarantees that increasing axial displacement increases
rotation. Condition (3) ensures the effective helix angle
remains strictly below $\pi/2$ and the geometric gain remains finite.

\begin{remarkbox}[Remark (Piecewise Profiles in Practice)]
The $C^1$ requirement above is sufficient for all derivations in this document.
In practice, piecewise $C^1$ profiles (continuous everywhere, continuously
differentiable except at finitely many junction points where left and right
derivatives exist) are also admissible for numerical simulation. At junction
points, one-sided derivatives provide the required slope values, and the
resulting force discontinuities pose no difficulty for standard ODE solvers.
However, large slope discontinuities can introduce local stiffness into the
system: the abrupt change in force gradients may require adaptive solvers to
reduce the timestep significantly near the junction, degrading computational
efficiency. In extreme cases, explicit integrators may struggle to maintain
stability without impractically small step sizes, and implicit or stiff-aware
solvers may be preferable.
\\

Derivations for common ramp and helix profiles are provided in
Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ramp-profiles}.
\end{remarkbox}


\subsection{Primary Axial Force Model}\label{sec:primary-axial}

The primary axial clamping force arises from the competition between
centrifugal loading of the flyweights and the restoring force of the
primary compression spring.

As shown in \Fig{fig:primary_axial_mechanism}, each flyweight of mass
$m_f$ rotates with angular speed $\omega_p$ at an instantaneous radius
$r_{f,0}+r_f(s)$, where $r_{f,0}$ is the flyweight radius in the
reference configuration $s=0$ and $r_f(s)$ is the additional radial
displacement induced by the ramp. The resulting outward centrifugal
force is redirected by the ramp geometry described in
\Sec{sec:ramp-geometry} into an axial closing force on the movable
primary sheave.

Opposing this motion is the primary compression spring, characterized
by stiffness $k_p$ and preload $x_{p,0}$. As the sheaves close
($s$ increases), the spring compression increases, producing a larger
restoring force that resists further closure.

The resulting primary axial force is therefore the ramp-redirected
centrifugal contribution minus the spring force. This quantity,
denoted $F_{p,ax}(s,\omega_p)$, provides the primary-side contribution
to the generalized axial force used in the shift equation of motion.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/primary_cvt_mechanisms.png}
\caption{Primary CVT axial-force mechanism. Each flyweight of mass $m_f$
rotates at angular speed $\omega_p$ and experiences an outward
centrifugal force at total radius $r_{f,0}+r_f(s)$. The ramp geometry
redirects this radial loading into an axial closing force on the
movable primary sheave in the $+s$ direction, while the primary
compression spring $(k_p,\;x_{p,0})$ provides an opposing restoring
force. The lower flyweight--ramp pair is symmetric and shown unlabeled
for clarity.}
\label{fig:primary_axial_mechanism}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Centrifugal Loading of Flyweights}

From \theoryRef{th:centrifugal_force},

\[
F_c = m \omega^2 r.
\]

Let
\begin{itemize}
    \item $m_f$ denote the flyweight mass,
    \item $r_{f,0}$ denote the initial flyweight radius at $s=0$,
    \item $r_f(s)$ denote the incremental radial change produced by the ramp.
\end{itemize}

The total effective flyweight radius is therefore

\[
r_{f,\text{tot}}(s)
=
r_{f,0} + r_f(s).
\]

The radial centrifugal force is

\begin{equation}
F_{p,\mathrm{rad}}(s,\omega_p)
=
m_f \omega_p^2 \big(r_{f,0} + r_f(s)\big).
\label{eq:primary_radial}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Radial-to-Axial Projection}

The ramp redirects radial force into axial force.
As shown in \Fig{fig:primary_ramp_centrifugal_force},
the axial component of a force acting along the ramp is obtained by

\begin{equation}
F_{p,\mathrm{axial\;component}}
=
F_{p,\mathrm{rad}} \tan\alpha_p(s).
\label{eq:primary_projection}
\end{equation}

From \Eq{eq:alpha_primary_from_slope}, the ramp angle satisfies

\[
\tan\alpha_p(s) = \frac{dr_f}{ds}.
\]

Substituting gives

\begin{equation}
F_{p,\mathrm{cent}}(s,\omega_p)
=
m_f \omega_p^2 \big(r_{f,0} + r_f(s)\big)
\frac{dr_f}{ds}.
\label{eq:primary_centrifugal_axial}
\end{equation}

Thus the axial gain of centrifugal force is governed
by the local ramp slope, while the centrifugal magnitude
depends on the total flyweight radius.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/primary_ramp_centrifugal_force.png}
\caption{Primary ramp geometry. Radial centrifugal force is redirected
into axial force through the ramp. The projection introduces a factor
of $\tan\alpha_p$, which equals $dr_f/ds$ by definition of the ramp slope.}
\label{fig:primary_ramp_centrifugal_force}
\end{figure}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Primary Compression Spring}

From \theoryRef{th:hooke_linear},

\[
F = k \Delta x.
\]

Let $k_p$ denote primary stiffness and $x_{p,0}$ preload.

\begin{equation}
F_{p,\mathrm{spring}}(s)
=
k_p (x_{p,0}+s).
\label{eq:primary_spring}
\end{equation}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Net Primary Axial Force}

The net axial force generated by the primary mechanism is the difference between
the centrifugal axial component and the spring force:

\bigeq{eq:primary_axial}{Primary Axial Force}{
F_{p,\mathrm{ax}}(s,\omega_p)
=
m_f \omega_p^2 \big(r_{f,0} + r_f(s)\big)\frac{dr_f}{ds}
-
k_p (x_{p,0}+s).
}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Secondary Axial Force Model}\label{sec:secondary-axial}

The secondary pulley generates axial clamping force in two ways. First, the
secondary spring directly presses the sheaves together through axial compression.
Second, the helix mechanism converts torque carried by the secondary pulley into
additional axial force. The secondary is therefore torque-reactive: larger
transmitted torque can increase the clamping force applied to the belt.

The goal of this section is to derive the net secondary axial force
\(F_{s,\mathrm{ax}}\). The derivation follows the physical mechanism directly:
determine the torque reacted through the helix, convert that reacted torque into
axial force through the helix geometry, and then add the direct compression
spring force.

The secondary assembly consists of a fixed sheave, a movable sheave, a helical
slot between them, and a spring. The fixed sheave is attached to the secondary
shaft. The movable sheave can translate axially, but the helix constrains that
translation to occur with relative rotation between the movable and fixed
sheaves. Under \AssRef{assump:rigid_components}, this coupled motion is treated
as a rigid geometric constraint.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/secondary_3d_model.png}
\caption{Secondary CVT assembly showing the helix mechanism and spring. The
helix is modeled as a captured slot, so axial displacement of the movable sheave
is coupled to relative rotation between the movable and fixed sheaves.}
\label{fig:secondary_cvt_3d}
\end{figure}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Physical Role of the Helix}

The helix acts like a screw-like constraint between the fixed and movable
secondary sheaves. A screw produces axial force only when relative rotation is
resisted. The same idea applies here: torque applied to the movable sheave tends
to rotate it relative to the fixed sheave. Because the movable sheave is
captured by the helix, that relative rotation is tied to axial motion. The helix
therefore reacts torque, and the inclined helix path converts that reacted
torque into axial force.

This gives the structure of the force model. The helix contribution is not
determined by secondary speed alone. It is determined by the torque that the
movable sheave attempts to pass through the helix, after accounting for the
torque required to accelerate the movable sheave itself.

Let \(\tau_s\) denote the total signed torque transmitted from the belt to the
secondary pulley. Under \AssRef{assump:secondary_face_torque_split}, this torque
is treated as equally shared between the fixed and movable secondary sheave
faces. The belt-applied torque acting on the movable sheave is therefore

\begin{equation}
\tau_{b,M}
=
\frac{\tau_s}{2}.
\label{eq:secondary_movable_belt_torque}
\end{equation}

This is the portion of the belt torque that appears on the movable-sheave free
body and can therefore load the helix.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Movable-Sheave Kinematics}

The helix geometry was parameterized earlier by the relative rotation mapping

\begin{equation}
\theta_s = \theta_s(s),
\label{eq:secondary_helix_constraint_recalled}
\end{equation}

as introduced in \Eq{eq:secondary_theta_profile}. Here, \(\theta_s\) is the
relative rotation between the movable and fixed secondary sheaves.

The fixed sheave is attached to the secondary shaft, so its angular velocity is

\begin{equation}
\omega_F = \omega_s .
\label{eq:secondary_fixed_sheave_speed}
\end{equation}

The movable sheave does not generally rotate at this same speed while the CVT is
shifting. With the sign convention used here, positive helix rotation means that
the movable sheave lags the fixed sheave, so

\begin{equation}
\dot{\theta}_s
=
\omega_s-\omega_M .
\label{eq:secondary_relative_speed}
\end{equation}

Since the helix angle is a function of the shift coordinate,

\begin{equation}
\dot{\theta}_s
=
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\dot{s}.
\label{eq:secondary_theta_dot}
\end{equation}

Combining these two relations gives the angular velocity of the movable sheave:

\begin{equation}
\omega_M
=
\omega_s
-
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\dot{s}.
\label{eq:secondary_movable_omega}
\end{equation}

Thus, secondary shift does not only move the sheave axially. It also changes the
movable sheave's angular speed relative to the fixed sheave by an amount set by
the helix slope.

Differentiating \Eq{eq:secondary_movable_omega} gives

\begin{equation}
\dot{\omega}_M
=
\dot{\omega}_s
-
\frac{d^2\theta_s}{ds^2}\dot{s}^2
-
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\ddot{s}.
\label{eq:secondary_movable_alpha}
\end{equation}

This acceleration is needed because torque applied to the movable sheave has two
possible roles: it can be reacted through the helix, or it can angularly
accelerate the movable sheave.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Torque Reacted Through the Helix}

The movable sheave is now treated as a rotating rigid body. Under
\AssRef{assump:lumped_inertia}, its inertia about the secondary shaft is
represented by the scalar \(I_M\).

The torques acting on the movable sheave are the belt-applied torque
\(\tau_s/2\), the torsional spring torque, and the torque applied by the helix.
The secondary spring is twisted by the same relative sheave rotation that is
imposed by the helix. With torsional stiffness \(k_{s,\theta}\) and preload
deflection \(\theta_{s,0}\), the spring torque is

\begin{equation}
\tau_{s,\mathrm{spring}}(s)
=
k_{s,\theta}
\big(
\theta_{s,0}+\theta_s(s)
\big).
\label{eq:secondary_torsion}
\end{equation}

Let \(\tau_{\mathrm{helix}\rightarrow M}\) denote the torque applied by the
helix to the movable sheave. Applying \theoryRef{th:newton-rot} to the movable
sheave gives

\begin{equation}
\frac{\tau_s}{2}
+
\tau_{s,\mathrm{spring}}
+
\tau_{\mathrm{helix}\rightarrow M}
=
I_M\dot{\omega}_M .
\label{eq:secondary_rotational_n2l}
\end{equation}

The quantity needed for axial clamping is the equal and opposite torque applied
by the movable sheave into the helix:

\begin{equation}
\tau_{M\rightarrow\mathrm{helix}}
=
-\tau_{\mathrm{helix}\rightarrow M}.
\label{eq:secondary_helix_reaction_def}
\end{equation}

Solving the movable-sheave torque balance for this reacted torque gives

\begin{equation}
\tau_{M\rightarrow\mathrm{helix}}
=
\frac{\tau_s}{2}
+
\tau_{s,\mathrm{spring}}
-
I_M\dot{\omega}_M .
\label{eq:secondary_helix_reaction_torque}
\end{equation}

Substituting \Eq{eq:secondary_torsion} and
\Eq{eq:secondary_movable_alpha} gives the reacted helix torque in terms of the
state and acceleration variables:

\begin{equation}
\tau_{M\rightarrow\mathrm{helix}}
=
\frac{\tau_s}{2}
+
k_{s,\theta}
\big(
\theta_{s,0}+\theta_s(s)
\big)
-
I_M
\left[
\dot{\omega}_s
-
\frac{d^2\theta_s}{ds^2}\dot{s}^2
-
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\ddot{s}
\right].
\label{eq:secondary_helix_reaction_torque_expanded}
\end{equation}

This equation is the core of the secondary mechanism. The helix reacts the
movable-face belt torque and the torsional spring torque, except for the
portion of torque required to angularly accelerate the movable sheave.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Conversion from Reacted Torque to Axial Force}

The remaining step is geometric. The helix relates axial displacement to
relative rotation. For a virtual axial displacement \(\delta s\), the
corresponding relative rotation is

\[
\delta\theta_s
=
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\delta s.
\]

By virtual work, the axial force associated with the reacted helix torque is

\begin{equation}
F_{s,\mathrm{helix}}
=
\tau_{M\rightarrow\mathrm{helix}}
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}.
\label{eq:secondary_helix_force_general}
\end{equation}

A steeper torque-to-shift mapping therefore produces a larger axial force from
the same reacted torque.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/secondary_helix_torque_to_axial.png}
\caption{Helix free-body concept. Torque applied to the movable sheave is
reacted through the helix. Because the helix is inclined, the reacted torque
produces an axial force proportional to \(d\theta_s/ds\).}
\label{fig:secondary_helix_torque_to_axial}
\end{figure}

Substituting \Eq{eq:secondary_helix_reaction_torque_expanded} into
\Eq{eq:secondary_helix_force_general} gives

\begin{equation}
F_{s,\mathrm{helix}}
=
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}
\left[
\frac{\tau_s}{2}
+
k_{s,\theta}
\big(
\theta_{s,0}+\theta_s(s)
\big)
-
I_M
\left(
\dot{\omega}_s
-
\frac{d^2\theta_s}{ds^2}\dot{s}^2
-
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\ddot{s}
\right)
\right].
\label{eq:secondary_helix_force_expanded}
\end{equation}

The terms inside the brackets are the torque that the helix must react. The
factor multiplying the brackets is the helix slope that converts that reacted
torque into axial force.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Direct Compression Spring Force}

The secondary spring also contributes directly through axial compression. At
\(s=0\), the spring is pre-compressed by \(x_{s,0}\). As the secondary sheaves
open with increasing shift, the compression increases by \(s\). From
\theoryRef{th:hooke_linear}, the direct axial spring force is

\begin{equation}
F_{s,\mathrm{comp}}(s)
=
k_{s,x}(x_{s,0}+s).
\label{eq:secondary_comp_spring}
\end{equation}

This force exists even if no torque is being transmitted. It provides the
baseline secondary clamping contribution, while the helix contribution adds the
torque-reactive part.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Net Secondary Axial Force}

The net secondary axial force is the sum of the direct compression-spring force
and the helix-generated force:

\bigeq{eq:secondary_axial}{Secondary Axial Force}{
F_{s,\mathrm{ax}}
=
k_{s,x}(x_{s,0}+s)
+
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}
\left[
\frac{\tau_s}{2}
+
k_{s,\theta}
\big(
\theta_{s,0}+\theta_s(s)
\big)
-
I_M
\left(
\dot{\omega}_s
-
\frac{d^2\theta_s}{ds^2}\dot{s}^2
-
\frac{d\theta_s}{ds}\ddot{s}
\right)
\right].
}

This form mirrors the physical mechanism. The first term is the direct axial
spring force. The second term is the helix force: the torque reacted through
the movable sheave and spring is converted into axial force by the helix slope.
The inertial term appears because part of the movable-sheave torque is used to
accelerate the movable sheave rather than being reacted through the helix.

The secondary force therefore increases with axial spring compression,
torsional spring wind-up, and transmitted secondary torque. It is also affected
by the motion of the secondary sheave, because the helix couples axial shift to
relative rotation. This is why the secondary is a torque-reactive clamping
mechanism rather than a simple position-dependent spring.

\subsection{Belt Centrifugal Force on the Wrap}
\label{sec:belt-centrifugal}

In addition to the actuator and spring forces derived previously, the belt
itself generates centrifugal loading as it rotates around each pulley.
This section derives the resulting contribution to axial clamping force.

Throughout this section, let
\[
j \in \{p,s\}
\]
denote either pulley, where \(j=p\) corresponds to the primary and
\(j=s\) corresponds to the secondary.

From \theoryRef{th:centrifugal_force}, a mass \(m\) rotating at angular velocity
\(\omega\) at radius \(r\) experiences centrifugal force

\[
F_c = m\omega^2 r.
\]

To apply this to the belt wrapped around pulley \(j\), three quantities must be defined:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item the radius at which the belt mass rotates,
    \item the mass of belt material carried around the wrap,
    \item the angular velocity of that belt mass.
\end{enumerate}

Each is developed below.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{1. Radius at Which the Belt Mass Rotates}

Centrifugal force acts at the center of mass of the rotating belt element,
not at the outer or inner belt surface. The location of this center of mass
therefore determines the radius that must be used in the centrifugal force
calculation.

The belt cross-section is approximated as a trapezoid
(see \Fig{fig:belt_cross_section_placeholder}) with:

\begin{itemize}
    \item radial thickness \(h_b\),
    \item outer width \(b_{\mathrm{out}}\),
    \item inner width \(b_{\mathrm{in}}\).
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/belt_cross_section.png}
\caption{Trapezoidal belt cross-section. The outer width \(b_{\mathrm{out}}\) corresponds
to the surface away from the pulley axis; the inner width \(b_{\mathrm{in}}\) corresponds
to the surface closer to the axis. The radial thickness is \(h_b\) and the centroid is 
located at \(\Delta r_{\mathrm{cm}}\) from the outer surface.}
\label{fig:belt_cross_section_placeholder}
\end{figure}

The cross-sectional area of the trapezoid is

\begin{equation}
A_b
=
\frac{h_b}{2}\left(b_{\mathrm{out}} + b_{\mathrm{in}}\right).
\label{eq:Ab_trap}
\end{equation}

Let \(y\) denote radial distance measured inward from the outer surface,
so that \(y=0\) at the outer surface and \(y=h_b\) at the inner surface.
For a trapezoid with linear sidewalls, the local width varies linearly with \(y\):

\begin{equation}
b(y)
=
b_{\mathrm{out}}
+
\frac{b_{\mathrm{in}} - b_{\mathrm{out}}}{h_b}y.
\label{eq:belt_width_linear}
\end{equation}

The centroid location \(\Delta r_{\mathrm{cm}}\), measured inward from the
outer surface, follows from the area-centroid definition:

\begin{equation}
\Delta r_{\mathrm{cm}}
=
\frac{1}{A_b}
\int_0^{h_b} y\,b(y)\,\dd y.
\label{eq:delta_r_cm_def}
\end{equation}

Substituting \Eq{eq:belt_width_linear} into \Eq{eq:delta_r_cm_def} gives

\begin{align}
\int_0^{h_b} y\,b(y)\,\dd y
&=
\int_0^{h_b}
y\left(
b_{\mathrm{out}}
+
\frac{b_{\mathrm{in}} - b_{\mathrm{out}}}{h_b}y
\right)\dd y
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\int_0^{h_b} b_{\mathrm{out}} y\,\dd y
+
\frac{b_{\mathrm{in}} - b_{\mathrm{out}}}{h_b}
\int_0^{h_b} y^2\,\dd y
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\frac{h_b^2}{2}b_{\mathrm{out}}
+
\frac{h_b^2}{3}\left(b_{\mathrm{in}} - b_{\mathrm{out}}\right)
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\frac{h_b^2}{6}
\left(b_{\mathrm{out}} + 2b_{\mathrm{in}}\right).
\label{eq:centroid_integral_steps}
\end{align}

Dividing by the area from \Eq{eq:Ab_trap} yields

\begin{equation}
\Delta r_{\mathrm{cm}}
=
h_b\,
\frac{b_{\mathrm{out}} + 2b_{\mathrm{in}}}
{3\left(b_{\mathrm{out}} + b_{\mathrm{in}}\right)}.
\label{eq:delta_r_cm_trap}
\end{equation}

For pulley \(j\), let \(r_{j,\mathrm{out}}(s)\) denote the outer belt radius.
The radius at which the belt mass rotates is therefore

\begin{equation}
r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}(s)
=
r_{j,\mathrm{out}}(s) - \Delta r_{\mathrm{cm}}.
\label{eq:rj_cm_definition}
\end{equation}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{2. Mass of Belt Material on the Wrap}

To determine the centrifugal loading on pulley \(j\), we next determine
the amount of belt mass associated with an infinitesimal portion of its wrap.

Let \(\theta\) denote the angular coordinate measured from the center of the wrap,
so that the wrapped region on pulley \(j\) spans

\[
\theta \in \left[-\frac{\phi_j}{2},\;\frac{\phi_j}{2}\right],
\]

where \(\phi_j(s)\) is the total wrap angle on pulley \(j\)
(see \Fig{fig:belt_centrifugal_wrap_placeholder}).

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{./illustrations/pulleyForces/infinitesimal_belt_element.png}
\caption{Belt wrapped around pulley \(j\). A differential element at angle \(\theta\)
has arc length \(\dd\ell = r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}\,\dd\theta\) and experiences radially
outward centrifugal force.}
\label{fig:belt_centrifugal_wrap_placeholder}
\end{figure}

A differential belt element subtending angle \(\dd\theta\) at the
mass-centroid radius \(r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}\) has arc length

\[
\dd \ell = r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}\,\dd\theta.
\]

Since the belt has cross-sectional area \(A_b\), the differential belt
element has volume

\begin{equation}
\dd V_j
=
A_b\,\dd\ell.
\label{eq:dV_belt_wrap_j}
\end{equation}

Substituting \(\dd\ell = r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}\,\dd\theta\) gives

\begin{equation}
\dd V_j
=
A_b\,r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}\,\dd\theta.
\label{eq:dV_belt_wrap_j_expanded}
\end{equation}

Let \(\rho_b\) denote the belt material density
(\si{\kilogram\per\meter\cubed}). The corresponding differential mass is then

\begin{align}
\dd m_j
&=
\rho_b\,\dd V_j
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\rho_b A_b\,r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}\,\dd\theta.
\label{eq:dm_belt_wrap_j}
\end{align}

This gives the amount of belt material associated with an infinitesimal
portion of the wrap on pulley \(j\).

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{3. Angular Velocity of the Belt Mass}

The rotating belt mass on pulley \(j\) must also be assigned an angular speed.
From \Sec{sec:state}, \(v_b\) denotes the belt transport speed. 
The corresponding angular velocity of the belt mass centroid
on pulley \(j\) is then

\begin{equation}
\omega_{b,j}
=
\frac{v_b}{r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}(s)}.
\label{eq:omega_bj_definition}
\end{equation}

This relation expresses the fact that the same belt transport speed corresponds
to different local angular velocities depending on the radius of motion. 

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Distributed Centrifugal Load Along the Wrap}

With the radius, differential mass, and angular velocity now defined,
the centrifugal force on the differential belt element follows from
\theoryRef{th:centrifugal_force}:

\begin{align}
\dd F_{c,j}
&=
\dd m_j\,\omega_{b,j}^2\,r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\left(\rho_b A_b\,r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}\,\dd\theta\right)
\left(\frac{v_b}{r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}}\right)^2
r_{j,\mathrm{cm}}
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\rho_b A_b\,v_b^2\,\dd\theta.
\label{eq:dFc_belt_j}
\end{align}

This force acts radially outward at each point along the wrap.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Total Radial Force Over the Wrap}

Integrating \Eq{eq:dFc_belt_j} over the full wrap angle gives the total
radially-directed centrifugal force on pulley \(j\):

\begin{align}
F_{c,j,\mathrm{rad}}
&=
\int_{-\phi_j/2}^{\phi_j/2}
\rho_b A_b\,v_b^2\,\dd\theta
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\rho_b A_b\,v_b^2
\int_{-\phi_j/2}^{\phi_j/2}\dd\theta
\nonumber\\[6pt]
&=
\rho_b A_b\,v_b^2\,\phi_j(s).
\label{eq:Fc_rad_wrap_j}
\end{align}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Radial-to-Axial Conversion Through the Sheave Cone}

The radial centrifugal force acts outward against the conical sheave faces.
Because the sheaves are inclined at cone half-angle \(\beta\), radial loading
cannot occur without simultaneous axial motion of the sheaves.
Thus, a purely radial force applied to the belt generates an axial
reaction component that contributes to clamping.

From the pulley geometry derived previously (see \Eq{eq:cone_geometry_diff}),
the relationship between radial and axial displacement in the active
shift region is

\[
\frac{\dd r}{\dd s}
=
\frac{1}{2\tan\beta}.
\]

For an infinitesimal displacement,

\[
\delta W
=
F_{\mathrm{rad}}\,\dd r
=
F_{\mathrm{ax}}\,\dd s.
\]

Substituting \(\dd r = \dfrac{1}{2\tan\beta}\dd s\) gives

\[
F_{\mathrm{rad}}\,
\frac{1}{2\tan\beta}\dd s
=
F_{\mathrm{ax}}\,\dd s.
\]

Canceling \(\dd s\) yields

\begin{equation}
F_{\mathrm{ax}}
=
\frac{F_{\mathrm{rad}}}{2\tan\beta}.
\label{eq:radial_to_axial_conversion}
\end{equation}

Thus, the axial clamping contribution from belt centrifugal force on pulley \(j\) is

\begin{equation}
F_{c,j}
=
\frac{
\rho_b A_b\,v_b^2\,\phi_j(s)
}{
2\tan\beta
}.
\label{eq:Fcj_general}
\end{equation}

% ==============================================================
\subsection{Axial Shift Dynamics}\label{sec:shift-dyn}

The previous subsections derived the axial forces produced by the primary and
secondary pulley mechanisms. The remaining task is to use those forces to
determine the motion of the shift coordinate \(s(t)\).

The shift coordinate represents coordinated motion of several physical bodies.
As \(s\) increases, the primary movable sheave closes, the secondary movable
sheave opens through the belt-length constraint, and belt material migrates
through the pulley grooves. The axial equation of motion is therefore written
in the generalized coordinate \(s\), with all force and inertia terms expressed
in that coordinate.

From the axial force balance introduced in \Eq{eq:axial_newton_base},

\begin{equation}
m\,\ddot{s}
=
F_{p,\mathrm{ax}} - F_{s,\mathrm{ax}},
\label{eq:axial_newton_base_recall}
\end{equation}

where \(F_{p,\mathrm{ax}}\) acts in the positive \(s\) direction and
\(F_{s,\mathrm{ax}}\) opposes it. This section replaces the placeholder mass
\(m\) with the equivalent inertia associated with the shift coordinate and then
adds the axial belt centrifugal contributions derived in \Sec{sec:belt-centrifugal}.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Equivalent Inertia in the Shift Coordinate}

Since \(s\) is a generalized coordinate, the inertia associated with shift is
constructed from kinetic energy. Following \theoryRef{th:kinetic_energy_trans},
the translational kinetic energy associated with shift is written as

\[
T_{\mathrm{shift}}
=
\frac{1}{2}m_{\mathrm{eq}}\dot{s}^{2}.
\]

The movable primary sheave translates with speed \(\dot{s}\). The movable
secondary sheave translates with the same speed magnitude in the opposite
physical direction, so its kinetic energy also contributes to the same
generalized coordinate. Let \(m_{p,m}\) and \(m_{s,m}\) denote the translating
masses of the primary and secondary movable sheaves.

The belt also contributes to the shift inertia. Under
\AssRef{assump:constant_belt_length}, the total belt mass is constant and is

\begin{equation}
m_b
=
\rho_b A_b L_b,
\label{eq:mb_def}
\end{equation}

where \(A_b\) is the trapezoidal belt cross-sectional area from
\Eq{eq:Ab_trap}. Under \AssRef{assump:single_belt_speed}, the belt is represented
by a single lumped transport speed, while the simplified axial contribution
